--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score decreases by 1 for items from one half (GOOD half), increases by 1 for items from the other half (BAD half).
- We need robots to: go to the GOOD source, pick up items, return to nest, deposit.
- The GOOD half could be behind BLUE or GREEN walls - we don't know which is good initially, but we need to identify it.
- Floor colors: WHITE = nest, BLACK = source, GRAY = corridor between.
- Wall colors: RED = nest side, BLUE = one half of source, GREEN = other half.

**Key insight:** Since we're minimizing, we want items that decrease the score. One color (BLUE or GREEN) gives -1 items, the other gives +1 items. We need robots to go to the right half.

**Strategy:**
- Robots should go toward the source (BLACK floor), preferring one color wall (try BLUE or GREEN), then return to nest (WHITE floor).
- Use GoToColor to navigate toward colored walls/objects.
- Colors: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

**State designs:**
- State for navigating to source (GoToColor toward blue or green wall)
- State for returning to nest (GoToColor toward red wall)
- Use floor color transitions (BLACK = at source, WHITE = at nest)

Since we don't know which color is good, I'll try both BLUE (3) and GREEN (2) targeting, plus exploration-based approaches.

**Machine designs:**

1. **Elitism baseline** - 3 states: explore→source(blue)→return to nest
2. Try targeting GREEN instead of BLUE
3. Simple 2-state: go to source, go back
4. Mixed strategies with exploration

Let me design 14 machines:

- Machines 1-2: Target BLUE wall for source, RED wall for nest return
- Machines 3-4: Target GREEN wall for source, RED wall for nest return
- Machines 5-7: Exploration-based foraging
- Machines 8-10: Mixed with neighbor conditions
- Machines 11-14: Different topologies

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Since I don't have explicit scores from Round 2, I need to reason about what would work best based on mission analysis.

**Key insights:**
- We want robots to collect from the GOOD half (either BLUE=color 3 or GREEN=color 2 walls) and return to nest (RED wall=color 1, WHITE floor)
- GoToColor toward colored walls should navigate robots to the right source half
- Floor colors: BLACK=source, WHITE=nest, GRAY=transit
- The basic loop: go to source → pick up → return to nest → repeat

**What I know about the color encoding:**
- clr=1: red, clr=2: green, clr=3: blue, clr=4: yellow, clr=5: magenta, clr=6: cyan

**Best strategy refinements:**
1. Keep both BLUE-targeting and GREEN-targeting variants (we don't know which is good)
2. The transition when ON BLACK floor (at source) should trigger return behavior
3. The transition when ON WHITE floor (at nest) should trigger go-to-source behavior
4. Add GoAwayColor from the BAD half while at source to prefer the good half

**Improved designs:**
- 3-state machines: (1) go to source color, (2) wait/explore briefly at source, (3) return to red/nest
- Try GoAwayColor from green while going to blue, and vice versa
- Add probability tuning on transitions

Let me create 14 diverse machines covering both color hypotheses with varied topologies:

# ROUND 4/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 3 rounds without explicit score feedback, I need to think more carefully about what's likely working and what isn't.

**Mission re-analysis:**
- Score decreases by 1 for items from the GOOD half, increases by 1 for BAD half
- We want to minimize → collect from GOOD half only
- The challenge: we don't know if BLUE (clr=3) or GREEN (clr=2) is the good half
- Both should be tried, and the optimizer will select the better one

**Critical design issues I may have missed:**
1. When a robot enters a source half, it automatically gets that item - no "waiting" needed
2. The robot needs to then navigate back to WHITE floor (nest) to deliver
3. After delivery, go back to source
4. Key: avoid the BAD half entirely

**Better state machine logic:**
- State 0 (GoToColor→BLUE or GREEN): navigate to good source. Transition: when on BLACK floor → State 1
- State 1 (GoToColor→RED wall): return to nest. Transition: when on WHITE floor → State 0
- This 2-state machine is clean and direct

**Why my previous machines may underperform:**
- The intermediate "explore" state wastes time
- Transition probabilities <1.0 on floor-color conditions mean robots sometimes don't switch when they should
- The floor color conditions (0,1,2) should probably have high probability (0.9-1.0)

**New approach:**
- Simple 2-state: GoToColor(source_color) ↔ GoToColor(RED)
- 3-state with avoid bad color: GoToColor(good) → return → avoid bad while searching
- Try GoAwayColor from bad half to steer away

Let me also reconsider: maybe exploration is needed to find the colored walls. GoToColor uses the camera to find colored objects. If robots can see the colored walls from far away, GoToColor should work directly.

**For 14 machines:**
- 2 elite copies (best guesses from round 2 - both BLUE and GREEN targeting, simple 2-state)
- 4 variants with probability tuning
- 4 with 3-state designs
- 4 with 4-state/alternative designs

# ROUND 5/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 4 rounds without explicit score feedback, I need to reconsider my approach more fundamentally.

**Core problem re-analysis:**
- The scoring says one half gives -1 (good) and the other gives +1 (bad)
- We need robots to preferentially visit the GOOD half
- The walls behind one half are BLUE (clr=3), the other GREEN (clr=2)
- We don't know which is good a priori - the optimizer will select the better color

**What might be failing:**
1. GoToColor moves toward colored OBJECTS seen by the camera. The colored walls should be visible from the arena. This should work for navigation.
2. But: when a robot is in the source area on BLACK floor, it already has an item. It should immediately start returning. The transition `on BLACK → go to RED wall` makes sense.
3. When on WHITE floor → item delivered → go back to source.

**Critical insight I may have been missing:**
- The simple 2-state machine (GoToBlue ↔ GoToRed) is probably the strongest design
- Transition: BLACK floor → switch to GoToRed; WHITE floor → switch to GoToBlue
- This creates a clean foraging loop

**But there's a subtlety:** When going to source, the robot might enter the WRONG (bad) half first. We want to specifically go to ONE colored half. GoToColor toward BLUE should steer toward the BLUE wall side.

**New ideas to try:**
1. The robot in "return" state might benefit from GoAwayColor(black floor side) rather than GoToColor(red)
2. Maybe use neighbor-based coordination
3. Try GoToColor toward the source color but with GoAwayColor from the other source color as an intermediate state to steer to correct half

**14 machines for round 5:**
- 4 simple 2-state machines (best bets, both colors, slight variations)
- 4 clean 3-state machines  
- 3 with GoAwayColor variants
- 3 exploration-heavy alternatives

## Reasoning

After 5 rounds without explicit score feedback, I need to think about this more carefully. The lack of feedback means I'm essentially blind, so I should diversify more aggressively while keeping my best structural guesses.

**Fundamental re-examination:**

The 2-state machine (GoToColor[source] ↔ GoToColor[RED]) is theoretically the cleanest. Let me verify the logic:
- State 0: GoToColor(BLUE or GREEN) - moves toward source color
- Transition: when floor=BLACK → go to State 1 (we're at source, have item)
- State 1: GoToColor(RED) - moves toward nest wall
- Transition: when floor=WHITE → go to State 0 (delivered, go back)

This should work. But maybe there's an issue: GoToColor moves toward the NEAREST colored object. If multiple colored objects exist, the robot may not always go to the right place. Also, the RED wall is on the NEST side - robots should move toward it to return.

**Key uncertainty:** Which color (BLUE=3 or GREEN=2) is the GOOD source? The optimizer should be finding this out. I'll keep both.

**New ideas:**
1. What if the transition should use GRAY floor (not WHITE) for the "left nest" trigger? No - WHITE is nest, that's where delivery happens.
2. What about using `--c 7` (perceive color) instead of floor conditions? Perceiving BLUE/GREEN might be more reliable than waiting for BLACK floor.
3. What if robots need exploration first to find the colored walls?

**Alternative: Use camera perception to trigger transitions:**
- State 0 (Explore): random walk → when perceive BLUE → go to State 1
- State 1 (GoToBlue): move toward blue → when BLACK floor → go to State 2  
- State 2 (GoToRed): move to nest → when WHITE floor → go to State 0

**Also trying:** GoAwayColor from RED (to go to source) and GoAwayColor from BLUE/GREEN (to avoid bad half).

Let me try a broader variety this round including perception-triggered transitions and different movement primitives.

# ROUND 7/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 6 rounds with no score feedback, I need to think about what's fundamentally different about this problem and try more radical approaches.

**Re-reading the mission carefully:**
- Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, REPLACING any item already carried
- An item from one half decreases score by 1, from the other increases by 1
- We want to minimize → collect from the DECREASING half only

**Key insight I may have been missing:**
The robot replaces its item when entering any source half. So if a robot enters the BAD half after the GOOD half (while returning), it gets a BAD item! The robot must avoid re-entering any source half after collecting.

**Another insight:** GoToColor toward BLUE (clr=3) or GREEN (clr=2) wall - but the walls are BEHIND the source area. The robot needs to pass through the source to reach the wall. So GoToColor toward BLUE should naturally take the robot into the BLUE half of the source.

**The return path problem:** When returning (GoToColor toward RED wall), the robot must travel back through the source area. This means it might pick up a BAD item on the way back if it crosses into the wrong half!

**Better design:** After collecting (on BLACK floor), don't navigate back through the source. Instead use GoAwayColor from the source color (BLUE or GREEN) to push away from source, then GoToColor toward RED.

Or: After collecting, GoAwayColor(BLACK floor isn't a color...) - can't do that.

**Alternative:** Use GoToColor(RED) aggressively - the RED wall is on the NEST side (opposite end from source). When on BLACK floor, switch to GoToColor(RED) which should pull the robot directly toward the nest, traversing the gray corridor and hitting the white nest floor.

The return path through source: actually if the robot is at the BLUE half and starts going toward RED wall (on the opposite end), it would naturally move away from source through the gray area. The BLUE/GREEN walls are at the far end, RED is at the near end (nest side). So the path: start at nest area → travel across gray → enter source (black floor) → get item → travel back (GoToRed) → cross gray → reach nest (white floor) → deliver.

The concern is: when traveling back, does the robot cross back into the source? It shouldn't need to if GoToRed pulls it efficiently toward the red wall.

**Maybe the real issue:** I've been trying the same basic designs. Let me try completely different approaches:

1. **Attraction-based:** Use robot LED colors to signal state, use attraction/repulsion for coordination
2. **GoAwayColor(RED)** to go TO source (away from nest), then **GoToColor(RED)** to return
3. **Very simple**: Just exploration that happens to collect randomly

Let me also try: What if the floor condition triggers are wrong? Maybe I should use GRAY floor (entering corridor from source) as trigger to start returning, not BLACK floor. The BLACK floor is the source itself - the moment a robot enters BLACK, it gets an item. So transitioning ON BLACK is correct.

**New approaches for round 7:**
- GoAwayColor(RED) to reach source + GoToColor(RED) to return
- Attraction-based swarming toward colored walls
- Try with GRAY floor conditions as additional triggers

## Reasoning

After 7 rounds with no explicit score feedback, I need to fundamentally reconsider my approach. The optimizer is running but I have no idea which machines are performing well. Let me think very carefully about what could be going wrong.

**Deep mission re-analysis:**

1. The score is MINIMIZED. Items from one half give -1 (good), from other give +1 (bad).
2. Robots start in the CENTER of the arena, not at the nest.
3. The nest is WHITE floor with RED wall. Source is BLACK floor with BLUE/GREEN walls.
4. A robot carries at most one item. Entering a source half REPLACES the current item.

**Critical problem with my designs:**
- GoToColor(BLUE) should guide toward the BLUE-walled half - BUT the walls are BEHIND the source. The robot would need to pass through the source to get close to those walls.
- When returning with GoToColor(RED), the robot heads toward the RED wall (nest side). This is correct.
- But: does the robot pick up a NEW item when re-entering the source on the way back? YES! If it crosses the black floor again while returning, it gets a new item (possibly from the wrong half).

**The fundamental navigation challenge:**
- Going to source: robot needs to go from center/nest → cross gray → enter black (gets item) → continue to wall
- Returning: robot in source → needs to go back through black floor → might get replaced item!

**Maybe the solution involves GRAY floor as a trigger:**
- When floor turns GRAY (leaving source area going toward nest), keep going to RED wall
- This avoids re-entering source on return path

**Alternative - try completely different approach:**
- Use GoAwayColor(RED) to go to source (away from nest wall)
- When on BLACK floor, switch to GoToColor(RED) to return
- The GRAY trigger: when floor=GRAY while returning, continue to RED

**What if I try avoiding the bad half by using GoAwayColor from it?**
- GoToColor(BLUE) + GoAwayColor(GREEN) simultaneously isn't possible in one state
- But: GoToColor(BLUE) already biases toward blue side

**Let me try a radically different set:**
1. Use GRAY floor condition to handle the corridor crossing
2. Use GoAwayColor(RED) as the "go to source" behavior
3. Try pure exploration with high transition rates
4. Try attraction to other robots carrying items (signaled by LED)

**New key insight:** Maybe I should use the LED colors to signal carrying state, and use camera perception (C=7) to detect robots in specific states for coordination.

Let me try 13 diverse machines with fresh ideas:

- Machines 1-2: Elitism - best simple 2-state (both color variants)
- Machines 3-4: GoAwayRed to source, GoToRed to return
- Machines 5-6: 3-state with GRAY floor as intermediate trigger
- Machines 7-8: 3-state with GoAwayColor from bad half as intermediate
- Machines 9-10: LED-based coordination using camera perception
- Machines 11-13: Alternative topologies

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 8 rounds with no score feedback, I'm clearly not getting information about what's working. Let me take a completely fresh, systematic approach.

**Fundamental re-read of the problem:**

The mission says:
- Score is MINIMIZED
- One half of source gives -1 per delivery (GOOD), other gives +1 (BAD)
- We don't know which color (BLUE/GREEN) is good
- BUT: the optimizer runs many simulations of each machine and selects the best

**Wait - key realization:** The optimizer evaluates EACH machine multiple times. If BLUE is consistently the good half (fixed per run or per session), then machines targeting BLUE will consistently score lower than machines targeting GREEN. The optimizer will find this out within its budget.

**So my strategy of providing both BLUE and GREEN variants is correct.** The optimizer will discover which is good.

**What if neither is working well?** Maybe my basic design is flawed. Let me trace through very carefully:

Starting position: robots scattered in CENTER of arena.
- Center = GRAY floor
- Nest = one end, WHITE floor, RED wall
- Source = other end, BLACK floor, BLUE/GREEN walls

**Trace of 2-state machine (GoToBlue → GoToRed):**
1. Robot starts in GRAY, state 0 = GoToColor(BLUE)
2. Robot moves toward BLUE wall (at source end)
3. Robot crosses BLACK floor → gets BLUE item → condition fires (C=0, BLACK floor) → switches to state 1
4. State 1 = GoToColor(RED), robot moves toward RED wall (at nest end)
5. Robot crosses WHITE floor → condition fires (C=2, WHITE floor) → switches back to state 0
6. Item delivered! Repeat.

This SHOULD work. The issue might be:
- When on BLACK floor, the transition probability matters. With p=1.0 it fires immediately.
- But condition C=0 fires when floor IS black - so robot immediately switches when entering source.
- Then GoToRed pulls it back through the arena.

**Potential issue:** When returning via GoToRed, robot must cross BLACK floor again (re-entering source). This might give it a new item (possibly from wrong half) BEFORE it reaches WHITE floor.

Actually wait - re-reading: "Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, REPLACING any item it was already carrying."

So if robot enters BLUE half, gets BLUE item, then on return crosses BLUE half again = gets BLUE item again (same type, OK). But if it crosses GREEN half = gets GREEN item (bad!).

**The path matters:** If GoToColor(RED) pulls the robot directly back through the arena center, it might cross either half of the source area. This is unpredictable.

**Better design for return:** GoAwayColor(BLUE) would push away from blue, but also away from source. Combined with GoToColor(RED)... but we can't combine behaviors.

**Maybe the solution is:** Use GoAwayColor from the source color (same color as collection) to exit the source area quickly, then when on GRAY, switch to GoToColor(RED).

Let me try a 3-state machine:
- State 0: GoToColor(BLUE) → on BLACK: go to State 1
- State 1: GoAwayColor(BLUE) → on GRAY: go to State 2  [exits source area]
- State 2: GoToColor(RED) → on WHITE: go to State 0 [returns to nest]

But wait - C=1 (GRAY floor) needs to be a transition condition. Let me use that.

Also trying: What if the issue is that robots start in center and GoToColor(BLUE) works fine initially, but I need to make sure they don't get stuck?

**New batch - 13 machines with fresh ideas:**

## Reasoning

This is the final round. I need to make my best possible 12 machines.

**Summary of what I've been doing:**
- Consistently providing both BLUE (clr=3) and GREEN (clr=2) targeting variants
- 2-state, 3-state, and 4-state machines
- Various combinations of GoToColor, GoAwayColor, Exploration

**Final strategic thinking:**

The core foraging loop should be:
1. Go to source (black floor) by navigating toward the colored wall
2. Get item (automatic on entering black floor)
3. Return to nest (white floor) by navigating toward red wall
4. Deliver (automatic on entering white floor)
5. Repeat

The 2-state machine is clean: GoToColor(BLUE/GREEN) → on BLACK floor → GoToColor(RED) → on WHITE floor → repeat.

**Key refinement for final round:**

The 3-state variant with GoAwayColor (same color as target) as intermediate state after collection should help robots exit the source area faster without accidentally entering the wrong half.

Pattern:
- State 0: GoToColor(BLUE) → BLACK floor → State 1
- State 1: GoAwayColor(BLUE) → GRAY floor → State 2  [exits source cleanly]
- State 2: GoToColor(RED) → WHITE floor → State 0

This is the cleanest design I haven't fully committed to. Let me make this the primary design.

Also: I've been using clr=1 for RED wall. Let me verify: color codes 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue. The nest wall is RED, so clr=1 is correct for GoToColor(RED wall).

**Final 12 machines:**
- 2 elite 2-state machines (both colors) - proven simple design
- 4 clean 3-state with GoAwayColor exit (both colors, two transition probability variants)  
- 2 3-state with explore intermediate
- 2 4-state comprehensive
- 2 alternative designs

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
